PREFACE TO THE LATIN TEXTS

Tue following version of the Hpistola Alewandri represents as
closely as possible the text of MS. C.C.C. Oxon. 82. No attempt
has been made to establish a critical text. The notes, accordingly,
have been reduced to a minimum, only such being given as are
sufficient to illustrate the relation of the MSS. In one or two
instances all available variant readings have been cited in order to
indicate the nature of the work necessary in the preparation of
a critical edition. The exact and restricted purpose has been,
however, to make accessible a hitherto unprinted text, which, in
unedited form, will better serve as a companion piece to the Old
English version than any edition compiled from numerous MSS.

MS. C.C.C. Oxon. 82 is a beautifully executed folio of the
twelfth century on vellum. It is written in two columns with
extremely wide margins, and, instead of the usual foliation, is
numbered by pages. The Hpistola Alewandri begins at page 156.
Two peculiarities mark the script : an indiscriminate use of ¢ and ¢
—preserved throughout the transcript—and a frequently pointless
employment of e with the cedilla. Only when this latter peculiarity
is obviously the graphic representation of the ligature x has it been
retained and the normal expansion made. The punctuation, being
meaningless, has been neglected. All contractions have been
expanded in italics.

It will be observed that throughout the Old English translation
occasional phrases and entire sentences of the Latin text are
omitted, and that, at the end, a long passage in the original is
completely ignored by the Old English scribe.

In the notes, MSS. are referred to by the abbreviations used by
Kiibler and Hilka, Through a hopeless confusion in the use of
these abbreviations the critical apparatus of Hilka’s edition is often
worthless. In such cases, reference is made, not to his variant
readings, but to his edited text. The abbreviations used in the
present edition are as follows:

K. _ Kiibler’s edition.

Nar. Cockayne’s text in his Varratiunculx.

78 Preface to the Latin Tests.

Ep. Jpistola Alexandr according to MS. C.C. C. Oxon. 82.
Ep’. LZpistola Alexandri as edited by Pfister from the Bam-
berg MS.

L Leyden, Voss. Q. 20.

M. Leyden, Voss. Q. 29.

H. Leyden, Bibl. Pub. Lat. 20.

G.  Guelf. Aug. 56. 16.

B Paris, Bibl. Nat. 8518.

P Paris, Bibl. Nat. 4880.

V. ‘Vienna, 150 (U. 863).

Mp. Montpellier, Fac. de Méd. H. 31.

a. Montpellier, Fac. de Méd. 384.

p- Paris edition of 1520.

bl. Basle edition of 1556.
Other abbreviations will be easily understood by reference to the
list of MSS. given at pp. xxxiv—xxxv of the Introduction.

The Latin version of the Wonders of the Hast follows the text of
MS. Cott. Tib. B v as closely as possible. All contractions are
expanded in italics. Capitalization and punctuation have been
preserved. Variant readings are recorded from Cockayne’s Var-
ratiuncule (C.) and from Knappe’s dissertation (K.).

The Vita Sancti Christophori fragment is reproduced from the
Acta Sanctorum for the 25th of July. Einenkel’s departures from
the text are recorded in the foot-notes. Attention is occasionally
called to the reading given by Boninus Mombritius (Momb.) in his
Sanctuarium sew Vitse Sanctorum.

bauimus quia intrari preter religionem & sine muneribus specus
non poterat. * Suplex orabam numina ut me regem totius orbis
terrarum cum sublimibus tropheis triumphantem in macedoniam
olympiade* matri mez remitterent. Quam rem frustra me petere
ita cognoui quontam dum sciscitor siquid etiam adhuc uidere
possem dignum admiratione indis id negantibus tune diuerti signa
in fasiacem iussi antinothi uenti flatum secutus. Tum itinere
sumpto agmen sub signis ducente me duo senes facti sunt nobzs
obuiam. Quos cum interrogarem numquid nossent in illa regione
dignum aliquid ad spectaculum responderunt michi esse uiam -x-
non amplius dierum per quam difficilis tamen esset ascensus *
propter aque penuriam proque tantis impedimentis si cum uni-
uerso pergere uellem exercitu. Ceterwm si commeatus -xl- - milia
hominum proponerem propter itinerum angustas semitas & bestiosa
satis loca posse contingere mthi ut aliquid incredibile perspicerem.
Tum ego letus * factus eos mulcens dicite inquam mihi quid sit
istud qwod michi illustre & tam magnificum pollicemini. Tum
uiri exhylarati blanda mea voce uidebis inquiunt*® rex quicumque
es duas solis & lune arbores indice & grece loquentes. Quarum
unum uirile robur est solis alterwm femininum est lune & ab his
que tibi instent* bona aut mala nosse poteris. Qua re tam in-
credibili illudi me a barbaris senibus existimans pena eos inpingi
& aliqua contumelia iussi notare ° illos dicens. Itane eo maiestas
mea peruenit ab occidente usque ad orientem ut a senibus bar-
baris ac decrepitis illudi posse uidear quibus iurantibus se nichil

posse falsi comminisci experire me modo posse

VITA SANCTI CHRISTOPHORI

Ego non sum stultus, sed sum servus Domini Jesu Christi; tu
autem stultus es, & insipiens, qui non confiteris Dominum Jesum
Christum, sed confiteris satanam patrem tuum. Et iratus rex
jussit ligari manus & pedes ejus, & ceedi ad virgas ferreas!: & mitti

5 in caput ejus cassidem igneam. Tunc dixerunt tres ex consulibus :
Beatus fueras Dagne, si natus non fuisses, quoniam talia tormenta
fieri jussisti Famulo Dei. Iratus rex jussit eos decollari.

Tune sanctus Christophorus dixit ad eum: Si amplius potueris
tormenta mihi facere, fac rex stulte: mihi vita eterna est, &

1o dulciora super mel & favum tormenta tua. Tune jussit rex fieri
scamnum ferreum secundum statum ejus. Et venerunt artifices &
tulerunt mensuram ejus, que erat cubitorum duodecim. Et factum
est secundum jussionem regis, & posuerunt eum in medium civitatis,

{ad f. 94>) [&] * jussit eum rex ibi ligari; & ignem supponi: & jussit quadra-

15 ginta? orcas olei mitti super eum. Respondit Sanctus Dei de
medio igne; & dixit: Hac tormenta tua, que mihi facis in tua
turpitudine, & diis tuis consumentur*. Ego semel tibi dixi: quia
non timebo tormenta tua, nec iram tuam. Cum hee dixisset de
multitudine flamme‘, scamnum illud factum est tamquam® cera.

20 Veniens autem rex, & videns sanctum Christophorum in medio
igne stantem & orantem (& erat facies ejus tamquam rosa nova)

[ad f. 95] videns eum rex, cecidit in faciem suam a timore magno * ab hora
prima usque ad horam nonam.

Postquam vero surrexit, dixit sancto Christophoro: Fera mala,

25 non tibi sufficiunt peccata animarum, quas errare fecisti, & non
permisisti sacrificare diis; sed omnem populum meum traxisti ad
te? Respondit sanctus Christophorus, & dixit: Adhuc multe
anime per me credere habent [in] Dominum Jesum Christum, & tu
ipse. Et blasphemavit rex eum: & dixit ad Sanctum Christo-

30 phorum Numquid & me vis in tuis maleficiis adducere? Ht iratus

Vita Sancti Christophort 109

valde rex dicit’ ad sanctum Christophorum: Sic mihi faciant dii

mei, & sic mihi reddant, si non crastina ista hora perdidero animam ?

tuam, & ad exemplum omnium te faciam pervenire. Alia autem

die jussit adduci sanctum Christophorum: & cum vyenisset ante
conspectum ejus, dicit ad eum: * Jam sacrifica diis & intellige [ad f. 95”)
verba mea, ut non per multa tormenta pereas. Sanctus Christo- 6
phorus dixit: Ego diis tuis abominationem feci; quia fidem meam
habeo, quam in baptismo accepi.

Tune jussit rex exhiberi* liignum magnum secundum statum
ejus, & poni ante palatium; & vocati sunt milites, & jussit famulum
Dei Christophorum ad lignum adligari. Et venientes milites
secundum ordinationem regis ternas sagittas sagittaverunt super
eum, ut citius interficeretur Famulus Dei. Et dixit rex: Videa-
mus, si Dominus ejus potest venire, & liberare eum de manibus
meis, & de sagittis his. Et sagittaverunt eum ab hora prima usque 15
horam duodecimam ; & putabat rex stultus quod tote * sagitte in
corpore ejus fixe essent. Sagittee autem suspendebantur a vento
a dextris atque sinistris ejus®: & nulla ex his corpus ejus tetigit.
Et post solis occasum, jussit eum rex dimitti ligatum, & custodiri
eum, * ne forte a Christianis nocte solveretur. Multus autem [ad f.96]
populus expectabat excipere corpus ejus. a1

Alio vero die dixit® rex: Eamus & videamus illum maleficum.

Et” veniens ad eum dixit ei: Ubi est Deus tuus? Veniat &
liberet te de manibus meis, & de sagittis his. Statim exsiliens®
una de sagittis ipsis ingressa est in oculum regis & exceecavit eum, 25
& dicit sanctus Christophorus: Tibi dico, tyranne stulte®, si credis :
ego crastino, hora octava, accipio meam coronam in omni boni.
Et hoc mihi Dominus ostendere dignatus est. Veniunt multi
Christiani, & accipiunt corpus meum, & ponunt illud in locum
orationis; tu autem veni in illo loco, & fac lutum cum sanguine in 30
nomine Domini nostri Jesu Christi; & pone in oculum tuum, * &”° [adf. 96°]
salvus fueris. Et tune adpropiavit hora, ut coronaretur Sanctus
Dei. Aperuit os suum in oratione & dixit:

110 Vita Sancti Christophori

Domine Deus meus, qui eduxisti me de errore in scientiam
hane*, quod te rogo, preesta mihi: & in quo loco posuerint corpus
meum*, non ibi ingrediatur grando, non ira flamme, non fames,
non mortalitas: & in civitate illa, & in illis locis, si fuerint ibi

5 malefici, aut demoniaci, & veniunt & orant ex toto corde, & propter

nomen tuum nominant nomen meum in suis orationibus, salvi

[ad f. 97] fiant. Et venit ei vox de celo dicens: Christophore, famule meus, *

ubi est corpus tuum, & ubi non est; commemorantur autem in

oratione sua nomen tuum; quidquid petierint, accipiant, & salvi

to fiant. Complens autem suum martyrim * bonum, coronatur mense
Julio vir Kal. Augustas.

Sunt autem numero, qui crediderunt in nomine Domini Jesu
Christi per sanctum Christophorum, millia hominum quadraginta
& octo, & anime centum undecim. Alia vero die dixit rex:

15 Hamus, & videamus, ubi posuerunt eum. Et veniens in illum
locum clamavit voce magna, dicens: Christophore, famule Dei,
[ad f. 97>] ostende mihi virtutem Dei tui, ut & ego credam ineum. E¢ tulit *
terram de loco illo, ubi passus est, cum modico sanguine ejus, &
posuit in oculum suum in nomine Dei Christophori; & in ipsa
20 hora aperti sunt oculi ejus. Tunc rex clamavit voce magna dicens:
Gloria tibi, Deus Christianorum ; qui facis voluntatem timentibus
te, & ego ab hodierno die [ponam] preceptum meum in omni
populo, & in omni lingua; [ut] quisquis blasphemaverit Deum
[ad f. 98] Christianorum, gladio percutiatur. * Hane orationem constituit
25 sanctus Christophorus: Domine Jesu Christe preesta bonam mer-
cedem scribentibus & legentibus passionem meam: qui regnas cum

Patre, & Spiritu sancto, in seculo seculorum‘. Amen.

NOTES

107 a/20. glengista. Ep: ut aliquid per nouarum rerum cognitionem
studio & ingenio possit accedere. Cockayne: Tota sententia male concepta
atque ordinata. Latinis ‘ possit accedere’ que respondeant in Anglicis deesse
videntur. Manifesto autem pro abedad legendum abedad, ‘ expostulet’. +++ °
Verbum autem bedan aliquando ut in Beowulfanis cogere, to compel significat,
quod Kemblius compellere dixit: cf. gebedan: neque tamen a biddan mul-
tum differt, ut poscere, postulare, significet,

108b/7. micelre gemynde. gemynd is usually neuter. Cf. 107b/19
and elsewhere.

109b/12. hon. Tendrils of a vine? Ep: in qua folia aurea racemique
cristallini & lichites [MSS : ligis] erant interpositi. Cockayne: Hon, Latine
ligis, hoe est, ni fallor, capreolis, claviculis, pampinis.

118a/15. The repetition is obviously merely a scribal error, due, probably,
to the recurrence of & ic.

118b/1. hrifra. Ep: serpentes & rabida [MSS. alii: rapida] ferarum
genera. Of. rifra, 121 b/12.

119a/7. Cockayne: of dele absurdum videtur pro ex parte mea. Healfe
hoe sensu usitatius.

119b/6. Cockayne: ic hie; deest ut videtur het.

119b/13. weefersien. Braun (Lautlehre Ep, Alex., p. 57) quotes the fol-
lowing from Biilbring: wafersien hat nach Ausweis des Vorkommens im
metrischen Psalter 68, 11 langes x, da der Vers nur nach (Sievers’) B-Typus
gelesen werden kann: dwt ic him wiefersyn. Das wird auch bestiitigt durch
die metrischen Stellen, wo wéfian vorkommt, das etymologisch denselben
Tonvokal hat. wafung und webbung haben dieselbe Bedeutung (‘ spectacu-
lum’, ‘scena’); das weist auf die urgerm. Ablautsreihe ¢, @ hin, und den
wgerm. Ablaut é, dé. Sweets Auffassung halte ich daher fiir falsch. Er hat
vermutlich ewdfian mit urgerm. ai angesetzt ; es ist aber zu beurteilen wie
slipan, ‘schlafen’ (mit Erhaltung des wgm. @ vor Labial+ Velarvokal) und
sldpian. Ahd. wabar-siuni muss daher auch mit @ gelesen werden.

120a/16. pyrste. Cockayne: pyrstan sensu causativo incredibile. Deest
igitur aliquid. The verb is used here impersonally with acc. of the person, as
also in other texts. Cf. Bosworth-Toller.

1214/7. Toller, in his Supplement to the A.S. Dictionary, does not accept
the reading ealfarena, Under Farw he states: iv. @ means of transport (2),
carriage or beast of burden :—ponne wes pridde healf pisend mila de pa
sedmas w@gon, and xxx, piisenda eal (a second has 1 been erased) farena and
oxna pi pe hw&te b&ron (quite xxx thousand carriages and beasts of burden
and oxen that carried wheat?). In the appendix to the Supplement, however,
Toller concurs with Sweet, who, in his Student’s Dictionary, gives calfara = a

112 Notes

camel; and with Napier (Contributions to O.E. Lexicography, Philological
Society’s Transactions, 1903-6, p. 342), who accepts both the word and its
etymology as given by R. Jordan in his Sdéugetiernamen. Jordan, says Napier,
‘suggested that it comes ultimately from the Arabic al faras, ‘‘ the horse”,
which found its way into Spanish as alfaras, the term for a horse of the
Moorish cavalry. It is also found in O.French auferan, and it is from this
Jordan derives O.E. ealfara. In the eleventh century the J had not yet
become uw in O.Fr.’ Cf. Nar., p. 71.

121a/20. The reading in me is given simply for want of a better. The MS.
may equally well be read mine or any other combination of letters that may
be made from an e preceded by six parallel down-strokes. im me certainly
falls short of being good Anglo-Saxon, but it seems best to agree with the Ep.
reading : Qu(a)e res me duppliciter torsit.

128 a/1o. The sentence construction is somewhat confused. Perhaps 7
should be omitted after geswencnissa in line 8, and 7 us in line 11 should be
emended to Jus. Of. 112 b/11 where a superfluous 7 is inserted.

123 a/13. Cockayne: Addidi & ut sit ure magis Latinum wri quam Angli-
cum. Cf., however, ure no ne ehton, 124 b/9. wri does not occur in the Latin
versions.

124a/15. carastis. A horned serpent. Cf. Isidorus Hispalensis (Bid/.
Patrum Lat., \xxxii, col. 444): Cerastes serpens dictus, eo quod in capite
cornua habeat similia arietum.

124b/1g. scluncon, An instance of epenthesis of a stop in the consonant
groupsl. Cf. K. Sisam in Archiv f. n. Sprachen, cxxxi, p. 305.

110a/19. gelicnisse ...heoramicle. MS. undecipherable. After -nisxe
is space for a letter, after which there seems vs. light to be an & followed by
three down-strokes (7 followed by two ?), Taking micle as the dat. sg. of
micelu, -c, f., Size, the passage may perhaps best be read: gelicnisse & in
heora micle. Ep: uulpibus similes.

110 b/r. Braun’s suggestion, ean steht wahrscheinlich fiir tan (Lautlehre
Ep. Alex.), is due to his having followed Baskervill’s reading.

110 b/5. nocticoraces. A Biblical word. Cf. Ps. cii. 6: Similis jactus
sum pellicano solitudinis ; jactus sum sicut nycticorax (Gk. text: vuxridpag)
in domicilio. The word occurs also in Lev. xi. 17, where Gk. text has
vuxtixopaxa, At fol. 143 of MS, Laud Misc. 247, in the Bodleian Library,
there is a pen-and-ink sketch of this bird, interesting in this connexion,

110 b/g. yb. The loss of medial m before labials occurs elsewhere.
Sievers (186, note) cites nybde for nymde from Ves. Ps, 194. 38.

110 b/15. flegdon. This word is most probably a unique spelling of the
rare verb fligan, fugare.

110 b/17. on morgen dwg. In M.L.N. 18, p. 246, Klaeber has the fol-
lowing remarks on this passage: Toller, in the Anglo-Saxon Dictionary,
approves of **** morgendeg, citing ** - from Cockayne’s edition. +++ + We
should read, however, on morgen deg, as is sufficiently proved by similar
passages. Thus, in the same text, line 714 [f. 129b/16] da on morgne, mid
Jy hit dagode. Vita Guthl. 40, 23 Da hit Ja on mergen dagian wolde ; ib, 22,
21 Da Jes on mergen mid pan hit deg wes (and accordingly ib. 58, 21 Ja hit
ja on mergen deg wes ; 86, 23); Bede 26, 23 hwader hit si Je xfenglommung

Notes 113

de on morgen deagung ; ib. 182, 28 pa hit ba wes on marne (BO Ca morgen)
dzxg geworden ; cf. ib. 174, 11 f. ; &e.—So in Chaucer, Cant. Tales, B 4215,
For on the morwe as soone as it was day.

On the other hand, morgendeg (mergende#g) = crastinus dies, as in Lind.,
Mt. 6, 34, Blickl. Hom. 213, 21 ff., Vita Guthl. 14, 28 is, of course, not to be
challenged.

112b/11. bic wes. Cockayne: } sepius ita ponitur, ut vix quidquam
sententiz conferre videatur. Cf. 1132/4; 116b/6.

118 a/6. gefyleea. Anally? Cf. B.-T. Suppl. Ep: factusque amicus ex
hoste macedonibus.

118/12. libri. An old Italian deity.

113b/19. cannon, Toller, in his Supplement, following Cockayne, says
cannon ‘seems to represent Lat. coenum’, the reading in Nar. from MS. Nero
D vit being : palus sicca et ceno habundans.

In Hilka’s edition, however, we have : Palus erat sicca et canna abundans ;
and in the Bamberg MS. the reading is: Invenimus paludem siccam, que
habebat cannas multas (Kleine Texte, p. 29). There can be no doubt, accor-
dingly, that the word represents the Lat. canna, -re; a reed, cane.

Kiibler (Rom. Forsch. vi. 205) has the following comment on the Lat. text :
Die Worte ... ‘ Palus erat sicca’ werden angefiihrt in der anonymen Schrift
de dubiis sermonibus (Keil, Gramm, Latin. v, p. 586). Leider ntitzt uns
dieses Citat wenig, da wir das Alter der Schrift de dubiis sermonibus nicht
kennen. Nur daswissen wir, dass sie friihestens im VII. Jahrhundert verfasst
sein kann, da Isidorus in ihr citiert wird. Of. Becker, Zur Alexandersage,
pp. 18-14.

114a/1o. Braun (Lautlehre Ep. Alex.) suggests that meahte ne should read
meahte we. The sentence, however, demands no emendation.

114 b/2. After swa full stop? Scribal omission of 2we ?

114b/12. rynig. MS: rynig, rying, or rymg. Cockayne: rymg, grun-
nitus, ex Latinis; est autem cum grymetan ‘grunnire’ cognatum et pro
+ grymg capiendum. Weber, Kyng Alisaundre, 5740. Jor hy ne haue so
mychel drade Of nothing as of hogges grade. (cry). Toller, citing
Covkayne, suggests rfung < rfan, and adds: Hriung (?) suspirtum, Wrt. Voc.
1.19, 34.

The word seems certainly to be here a derivative of the verb rfn, to roar
(ef. Ep : grunnitas), and may well be a variant of the unrecorded form rgning.
Cf. Biilbring, Hlementarbuch, 561: nach n+ Vokal schwindet Nasal vor
tautosyllabischen Konsonanten. Cf. cynige, f. 942/13, and cynigas, f.
103 a/11. On the other hand, however, there is no n in the root of rgn; 80
that the expected form, and the one perhaps to be read in our text, would be
roping.

115 2/6. ictifafonas. Ichthyophagi. This word gave the scribes great
trouble. Cf. Notes to Ep. for alist of the various readings.

116b/9. hos. I.e., hohas,a.pl. of koh. Cockayne: Ho. Dubium vix erit,
quin in hac voce adsit Scoticum Haugh, ‘ Low-lying flat ground properly on
the border of a river and such as is sometimes overflowed’. Jamieson. Mas-
culinum erit. Ep: promunctoria.

1172/14. meahte on. Cockayne: nominativus deest: an man? an ic?
Durissime tamen aliquando, ut in transitionibus, pronomen preetermittitur.

I

114 Notes

According to Pogatscher (Anglia, xxiii. 296), the suppression at this point
of the pronoun—which may, of course, be but a scribal slip—is an instance
of what he calls ‘ unausgedriicktes subject ’.

117 b/13. onhyscte. An instance of the ending -e for the subj. pl. Cosijn
(Altwestsachsische Gram. ii, pp. 120 ff.) enumerates thirty-one examples of this
ending, and Wyatt (O. H. Reader, p. 121, note to 40, 21) adds four more.
That there are ‘many more’ such examples, as Wyatt claims, is to be accepted
with caution.

126 b/12. hwite. Ep: dentibus caninis. Bamberg MS., ed. Pfister :
habebat nigrum corpus, dentes caninos.

127 a/10. The Bosworth-Toller citation of this passage silently alters the
MS. and the Nar. reading to the expected form of the g. sg., setlyonges.

127 b/16. instyred. Cf. onstyred, f. 112 b/12. Nothing in the Lat. text
will account for this spelling.

127b/18. smgde alexander. Nothing in the Lat. texts suggests this un-
fortunate interpolation. The scribe apparently forgot that he was translating
a letter.

128a/5. The text is certainly corrupt. Construction of sofre?

128b/9. Cockayne: efel expectabam wyrd. Probably a careless tran-
scription from the immediately preceding line.

100 a/12. conopenas. Cf. Berger de Xivrey, p. 252 : Et in Perside fingunt
esse bestias quas conopenos appellant, quibus, sub asininis capitibus, equina
dependet per cervices juba; et ore naribusque ignem flammasque exspirant.

101b/6. twa neb, ete. MS. Tib. B v: tQ neb 6n anum heafde bit $ cneo
swyde read.

101b/14. on drys heowes. Lat. text: homines tripartito colore. MS.
Tib. B v: preosellices. Cf. this word in B.-T, The s in drys is as
unaccountable as the genitive after on is ungrammatical. The meaning,
however, is unmistakable, and demands the reading, on Jrym heowum.

944/16. Hinenkel refers thus to Herzfeld’s reading: ‘H his heaf [de]
wovon gar nichts da steht!’ This, despite the reading of the MS. and the in
caput eius of the Acta SS.

95a/10. The uninflected participle preceding its noun is an unexpected con-
struction. Cf., however, Blk. Hom. (E.E.T.S. 73, p. 111) on urne drihten
hzelende crist.

95a/16. of pys gemynde & of pyssum life. The use here of Jys is ex-
ceptional. The normal case after of is the dative, as seen in of Jyssum life.
ys gemynde may, of course, be in the instr. case, which, after of, would be
irregular. Perhaps gemynde had better be taken as dative, and Jys accounted
for as a scribal error.

972/12. Read: eaht & feowertig? Cf. Acta SS.

97b/4. ic pis dem. Mombritius: In nomine domini nostri dei Christo-
phori credo.

GLOSSARIAL INDEX

The order of words in this Glossary is strictly alphabetical, the digraph x
being considered as two letters, and 4 (which is used throughout for @)
being placed after ¢. Words occurring in various forms are to be
sought under the spelling most nearly representing the W. Saxon norm.
Words found in one form only are so cited. The termination nis is in every
case changed to nes. Asa rule, the first example of a form is the only one
noted. Reference is to folio and line of the MS., the recto side of the leaf
being understood unless its number be followed by b. Such references without
an accompanying word mean that the form is to be found either in the head-
words or in the citation immediately preceding. C.and W. before a reference
indicate that the form is found in the Christophorus fragment or in Wonders
of the East respectively. The hyphen is used to replace the initial parts of
words. The present tense and the indicative mood are understood : 3 sg. =
pres. ind. act. 3sg. The parts of speech are not indicated after verbs and
nouns, such indication being superfluous. The abbreviations used are those
customarily employed in glossaries of this kind, and hardly need explanation :
pte. = present participle ; prep.w.d.a. = preposition with dative and accusative,
&c. Abbreviations of gender are preceded by those of case: 2.sg.n. =
nominative singular neuter ; &c. Finally, for convenience in locating forms,
such prefixes as ge and un have been indicated after their first occurrence by a
hyphen. The principal parts of verbs with such prefixes are to be sought, as
a rule, under the simpler forms.
